I guess… I’m here because I’ve been getting really overwhelmed since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business. Like, the art part is fine, mostly, but then there are invoices and client messages and all these little business things, and I just get so wound up about them.

I’ll tell myself I’m going to answer one email after I make coffee, and then somehow I’m reorganizing my brushes or looking up packaging ideas, and then it’s hours later. That’s kind of why I came in—since trying to make my art into a real freelance thing, these tasks keep turning into this whole spiral where I avoid them until I’m crying at the kitchen table or I forget normal stuff, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to focus or getting easily distracted by other things, even when you’re trying to do something that isn’t related to your freelance work, like chores or reading a book?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. But when it’s a client message or an invoice, or I have to sound professional, I get so wound up and then I put it off.\n\nAnd then I’ll do this whole thing where I tell myself I’m being productive because I’m, I don’t know, cleaning my art desk or looking at envelope sizes online, but really I’m avoiding the email. Then suddenly it’s late and I’m crying at the kitchen table because it feels like such a stupid thing to be stuck on. I’ve even forgotten normal stuff, like paying a bill, because I’m so caught up in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a sense of inner restlessness or tension when you're faced with these business tasks, like you can't sit still or focus until you get started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… it’s like I get wound up before I even open the message. I’ll know I need to do an invoice or reply to a client, and instead of just doing it I sort of buzz around doing other things, like rearranging supplies or checking some totally random business thing that doesn’t matter right then.\n\nAnd then it builds up, because I haven’t actually done the thing. That’s kind of why I came in—since trying to make my art into a real freelance thing, these tasks keep turning into this whole spiral where I avoid them until I’m crying at the kitchen table or I forget normal stuff, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to focus or getting easily distracted by other things, even when you’re trying to do something that isn’t related to your freelance work, like chores or reading a book?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I mean, everybody gets distracted doing chores, right? Like I’ll start folding laundry and then notice the plant needs water, and then I’m looking for the little watering can I bought at this thrift store—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it being a big problem when it’s just normal stuff like reading or chores. It feels more obvious when it’s the freelance business things. But I don’t know, maybe I’m not paying close enough attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your freelance work, do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like following through with detailed instructions or staying organized with everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really tracked it that way. Like, with everyday things, I can usually muddle through, I think? If there are a lot of steps I might get annoyed or wander off for a minute, but that feels kind of normal.\n\nI did recently try to organize this drawer with all my receipts and random batteries and, somehow, old birthday candles—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess outside the freelance stuff, it doesn’t jump out to me as a major problem. It’s more that I notice it when there’s some business-y task hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, even before you started your freelance business, did you often struggle with staying organized, starting tasks, or avoiding things that needed to be done?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like this. I mean, I’ve always had little messy piles and I’m not the most organized person—my desk has, like, three different mugs on it right now, which is embarrassing—but it wasn’t this whole overwhelmed, avoidant thing.\n\nIt really started maybe about a month after I took on that community arts contract and was trying to make the business more… official, I guess. Like once it felt formal and there were all these business tasks attached, that’s when I started freezing up and putting things off.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, even before starting your freelance business, did you ever notice that you would often lose things, forget daily tasks, or struggle to follow through on things you started?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in a way that stands out before all this. I’d forget little things sometimes, like anyone, and I’ve definitely misplaced my keys in weird places—once they were in a grocery bag with onions, don’t ask—but it didn’t feel like a pattern.\n\nThe overwhelmed, avoidant thing really started about a month after I took on that community arts contract and tried to make the business more formal. That’s when I started feeling like I’d freeze up around those tasks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty starting or finishing tasks that aren't related to your freelance work—like personal projects, hobbies, or even simple routines like getting ready in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little… I mean, the main thing is still the freelance stuff, but it sort of spills over. Like I put off replying to a client email for way too long, and then I felt awful about it, and at home I missed a utility payment, which is not really like me.\n\nI was trying to keep up with the art display at the same time, and everything just felt tangled together—like the email, the bill, the display labels, all of it. Personal routines aren’t impossible, exactly, but the responsibilities around home and work are getting harder to start or finish.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems managing daily responsibilities like personal hygiene, meals, or household chores because of how overwhelmed or distracted you feel?\n\nPatient: Um… hygiene and meals, not so much, I don’t think. Like, I’m still showering and eating, though sometimes dinner becomes crackers and cheese because I get distracted by one more thing—actually I bought this fancy mustard at the farmers market and now everything is “dinner” if it has mustard on it, which is probably not the point.\n\nBut the household and responsibility stuff, yes. It’s started affecting both the freelance work and home stuff. I put off replying to a client email way too long, and then I missed a utility payment while I was also trying to keep up with the art display. It’s like all the tasks pile up and I just… stall.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’d forget little things sometimes, like anyone, and I’ve definitely misplaced my keys in weird places—once they were in a grocery bag with onions, don’t ask—but it didn’t feel like a pattern.\n\nThe overwhelmed, avoidant thing really started about a month after I took on that community arts contract and tried to make the business more formal. That’s when I started feeling like I’d freeze up around those tasks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty starting or finishing tasks that aren't related to your freelance work—like personal projects, hobbies, or even simple routines like getting ready in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little… I mean, the main thing is still the freelance stuff, but it sort of spills over. Like I put off replying to a client email for way too long, and then I felt awful about it, and at home I missed a utility payment, which is not really like me.\n\nI was trying to keep up with the art display at the same time, and everything just felt tangled together—like the email, the bill, the display labels, all of it. Personal routines aren’t impossible, exactly, but the responsibilities around home and work are getting harder to start or finish.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems managing daily responsibilities like personal hygiene, meals, or household chores because of how overwhelmed or distracted you feel?\n\nPatient: Um… hygiene and meals, not so much, I don’t think. Like, I’m still showering and eating, though sometimes dinner becomes crackers and cheese because I get distracted by one more thing—actually I bought this fancy mustard at the farmers market and now everything is “dinner” if it has mustard on it, which is probably not the point.\n\nBut the household and responsibility stuff, yes. It’s started affecting both the freelance work and home stuff. I put off replying to a client email way too long, and then I missed a utility payment while I was also trying to keep up with the art display. It’s like all the tasks pile up and I just… stall.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your freelance work, do you find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require paying close attention, like following detailed directions or keeping track of multiple steps in a plan?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it’s kind of wrapped up in everything else. That’s when I started feeling like I’d freeze up around those tasks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty starting or finishing tasks that aren't related to your freelance work—like personal projects, hobbies, or even simple routines like getting ready in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little… I mean, the main thing is still the freelance stuff, but it sort of spills over. Like I put off replying to a client email for way too long, and then I felt awful about it, and at home I missed a utility payment, which is not really like me.\n\nI was trying to keep up with the art display at the same time, and everything just felt tangled together—like the email, the bill, the display labels, all of it. I’ve even forgotten normal stuff, like paying a bill, because I’m so caught up in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a sense of inner restlessness or tension when you're faced with these business tasks, like you can't sit still or focus until you get started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… it’s like I get wound up before I even open the message. I’ll know I need to do an invoice or reply to a client, and instead of just doing it I sort of buzz around doing other things, like rearranging supplies or checking some totally random business thing that doesn’t matter right then.\n\nAnd then it builds up, because I haven’t actually done the thing. That’s kind of why I came in—since trying to make my art into a real freelance thing, these tasks keep turning into this whole spiral where I avoid them until I’m crying at the kitchen table or I forget normal stuff, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to focus or getting easily distracted by other things, even when you’re trying to do something that isn’t related to your freelance work, like chores or reading a book?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I mean, everybody gets distracted doing chores, right? Like I’ll start folding laundry and then notice the plant needs water, and then I’m looking for the little watering can I bought at this thrift store—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it being a big problem when it’s just normal stuff like reading or chores. It feels more obvious when it’s the freelance business things. But I don’t know, maybe I’m not paying close enough attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your freelance work, do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like following through with detailed instructions or staying organized with everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really tracked it that way. Like, with everyday things, I can usually muddle through, I think? If there are a lot of steps I might get annoyed or wander off for a minute, but that feels kind of normal.\n\nI did recently try to organize this drawer with all my receipts and random batteries and, somehow, old birthday candles—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess outside the freelance stuff, it doesn’t jump out to me as a major problem. It’s more that I notice it when there’s some business-y task hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, even before you started your freelance business, did you often struggle with staying organized, starting tasks, or avoiding things that needed to be done?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like this. I mean, I’ve always had little messy piles and I’m not the most organized person—my desk has, like, three different mugs on it right now, which is embarrassing—but it wasn’t this whole overwhelmed, avoidant thing.\n\nIt really started maybe about a month after I took on that community arts contract and was trying to make the business more… official, I guess. Like once it felt formal and there were all these business tasks attached, that’s when I started freezing up and putting things off.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, even before starting your freelance business, did you ever notice that you would often lose things, forget daily tasks, or struggle to follow through on things you started?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in a way that stands out before all this. I’d forget little things sometimes, like anyone, and I’ve definitely misplaced my keys in weird places—once they were in a grocery bag with onions, don’t ask—but it didn’t feel like a pattern.\n\nThe overwhelmed, avoidant thing really started about a month after I took on that community arts contract and tried to make the business more formal. That’s when I started feeling like I’d freeze up around those tasks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty starting or finishing tasks that aren't related to your freelance work—like personal projects, hobbies, or even simple routines like getting ready in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little… I mean, the main thing is still the freelance stuff, but it sort of spills over. Like I put off replying to a client email for way too long, and then I felt awful about it, and at home I missed a utility payment, which is not really like me.\n\nI was trying to keep up with the art display at the same time, and everything just felt tangled together—like the email, the bill, the display labels, all of it. Personal routines aren’t impossible, exactly, but the responsibilities around home and work are getting harder to start or finish.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems managing daily responsibilities like personal hygiene, meals, or household chores because of how overwhelmed or distracted you feel?\n\nPatient: Um… hygiene and meals, not so much, I don’t think. Like, I’m still showering and eating, though sometimes dinner becomes crackers and cheese because I get distracted by one more thing—actually I bought this fancy mustard at the farmers market and now everything is “dinner” if it has mustard on it, which is probably not the point.\n\nBut the household and responsibility stuff, yes. It’s started affecting both the freelance work and home stuff. I put off replying to a client email way too long, and then I missed a utility payment while I was also trying to keep up with the art display. It’s like all the tasks pile up and I just… stall.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your freelance work, do you find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require paying close attention, like following detailed directions or keeping track of multiple steps in a plan?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it’s kind of wrapped up in everything else. Like, outside the actual freelance work, home responsibilities have started slipping too. I missed a utility payment, which made me feel really stupid because it was just sitting there, and I kept meaning to deal with it.\n\nAnd then with the art display, there were all these little pieces to keep track of, and at the same time I was avoiding replying to a client email. It’s like if there are multiple steps, my brain starts treating it like a junk drawer—actually I have one of those with batteries and old museum wristbands in it—and I just don’t know what to grab first. So yeah, it’s affecting both work and home stuff now.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, were you often distractible or had trouble staying focused on tasks or activities when you were a child, like in school or while doing homework?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember it being a big thing when I was a kid, but honestly my memory of school is kind of patchy in that way. I remember certain classrooms really clearly, like the smell of those dry-erase markers and this one teacher who had a huge collection of turtle figurines, which… not really what you asked.\n\nHomework, I mean, I probably procrastinated like a lot of kids did, but I don’t know if it was more than normal. Nothing really stands out to me as, “Oh, I was always distracted back then.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks or feeling stuck even when there’s no pressure from others or professional consequences, like personal errands or simple decisions such as what to wear or what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s showing up more when there are responsibilities stacked together, not just if someone is standing over me. Like I put off replying to a client way longer than I should have, and then I missed a utility payment, which was just sitting there needing to be done.\n\nAnd all of that was while I was trying to keep up with the art display stuff, which has all these tiny moving pieces—labels, timing, where things go, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I need better tape or if the frames are crooked. So I don’t know if it’s “simple decisions” exactly, but I do get stuck, and then things at home and with work start slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or have trouble staying still, even when you’re not facing any specific task or responsibility?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a big way. I mean, I shift around sometimes, like if a chair is uncomfortable or I’m waiting somewhere and there’s a weird clock ticking—there was one at my aunt’s house that was so loud it felt like it was arguing with the room—but I wouldn’t say I’m usually physically restless for no reason.\n\nI’m more… I don’t know, I can sit. Sometimes probably too much. So no, I haven’t really noticed trouble staying still like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it’s tied to that, but maybe more like I get overwhelmed first, and then I end up feeling really low after. I came in because ever since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business, I keep getting so wound up over things like invoices or client messages.

It’s stupid little stuff, or it feels like it should be little, like replying to someone or figuring out what to charge, and then I avoid it until I’m sitting at my kitchen table crying. And then I’ll forget normal things too, like paying a bill, because my brain is just stuck on this one client email. So, yes, the focus and getting things done part is happening with feeling down, but it feels like it starts from the stress of all of that.

I’ll tell myself I’m going to answer one email after I make coffee, and then somehow I’m reorganizing my brushes or looking up packaging ideas, and then it’s hours later. I’ve ended up crying at my kitchen table over it, which feels ridiculous. And I’ve been forgetting normal things too, like paying a bill, because I’m so tangled up in worrying about the client stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or avoidance is mainly around interacting with clients or presenting yourself professionally, or does it also happen with other types of social interactions?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the client and business side, I think. Like, if I’m just talking to a friend or chatting with someone at the grocery store, that’s not really the same. But when it’s a client message or an invoice, or I have to sound professional, I get so wound up and then I put it off.\n\nAnd then I’ll do this whole thing where I tell myself I’m being productive because I’m, I don’t know, cleaning my art desk or looking at envelope sizes online, but really I’m avoiding the email. I’ve even forgotten normal stuff, like paying a bill, because I’m so caught up in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a sense of inner restlessness or tension when you're faced with these business tasks, like you can't sit still or focus until you get started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… it’s like I get wound up before I even open the message. I’ll know I need to do an invoice or reply to a client, and instead of just doing it I sort of buzz around doing other things, like rearranging supplies or checking some totally random business thing that doesn’t matter right then.\n\nAnd then it builds up, because I haven’t actually done the thing. That’s kind of why I came in—since trying to make my art into a real freelance thing, these tasks keep turning into this whole spiral where I avoid them until I’m crying at the kitchen table or I forget normal stuff, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to focus or getting easily distracted by other things, even when you’re trying to do something that isn’t related to your freelance work, like chores or reading a book?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I mean, everybody gets distracted doing chores, right? Like I’ll start folding laundry and then notice the plant needs water, and then I’m looking for the little watering can I bought at this thrift store—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it being a big problem when it’s just normal stuff like reading or chores. It feels more obvious when it’s the freelance business things. But I don’t know, maybe I’m not paying close enough attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your freelance work, do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like following through with detailed instructions or staying organized with everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really tracked it that way. Like, with everyday things, I can usually muddle through, I think? If there are a lot of steps I might get annoyed or wander off for a minute, but that feels kind of normal.\n\nI did recently try to organize this drawer with all my receipts and random batteries and, somehow, old birthday candles—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess outside the freelance stuff, it doesn’t jump out to me as a major problem. It’s more that I notice it when there’s some business-y task hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, even before you started your freelance business, did you often struggle with staying organized, starting tasks, or avoiding things that needed to be done?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like this. I mean, I’ve always had little messy piles and I’m not the most organized person—my desk has, like, three different mugs on it right now, which is embarrassing—but it wasn’t this whole overwhelmed, avoidant thing.\n\nIt really started maybe about a month after I took on that community arts contract and was trying to make the business more… official, I guess. Like once it felt formal and there were all these business tasks attached, that’s when I started freezing up and putting things off.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, even before starting your freelance business, did you ever notice that you would often lose things, forget daily tasks, or struggle to follow through on things you started?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in a way that stands out before all this. I’d forget little things sometimes, like anyone, and I’ve definitely misplaced my keys in weird places—once they were in a grocery bag with onions, don’t ask—but it didn’t feel like a pattern.\n\nThe overwhelmed, avoidant thing really started about a month after I took on that community arts contract and tried to make the business more formal. That’s when I started feeling like I’d freeze up around those tasks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty starting or finishing tasks that aren't related to your freelance work—like personal projects, hobbies, or even simple routines like getting ready in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little… I mean, the main thing is still the freelance stuff, but it sort of spills over. Like I put off replying to a client email for way too long, and then I felt awful about it, and at home I missed a utility payment, which is not really like me.\n\nI was trying to keep up with the art display at the same time, and everything just felt tangled together—like the email, the bill, the display labels, all of it. Personal routines aren’t impossible, exactly, but the responsibilities around home and work are getting harder to start or finish.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems managing daily responsibilities like personal hygiene, meals, or household chores because of how overwhelmed or distracted you feel?\n\nPatient: Um… hygiene and meals, not so much, I don’t think. Like, I’m still showering and eating, though sometimes dinner becomes crackers and cheese because I get distracted by one more thing—actually I bought this fancy mustard at the farmers market and now everything is “dinner” if it has mustard on it, which is probably not the point.\n\nBut the household and responsibility stuff, yes. It’s started affecting both the freelance work and home stuff. I put off replying to a client email way too long, and then I missed a utility payment while I was also trying to keep up with the art display. It’s like all the tasks pile up and I just… stall.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your freelance work, do you find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require paying close attention, like following detailed directions or keeping track of multiple steps in a plan?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it’s kind of wrapped up in everything else. Like, outside the actual freelance work, home responsibilities have started slipping too. I missed a utility payment, which made me feel really stupid because it was just sitting there, and I kept meaning to deal with it.\n\nAnd then with the art display, there were all these little pieces to keep track of, and at the same time I was avoiding replying to a client email. It’s like if there are multiple steps, my brain starts treating it like a junk drawer—actually I have one of those with batteries and old museum wristbands in it—and I just don’t know what to grab first. So yeah, it’s affecting both work and home stuff now.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, were you often distractible or had trouble staying focused on tasks or activities when you were a child, like in school or while doing homework?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember it being a big thing when I was a kid, but honestly my memory of school is kind of patchy in that way. I remember certain classrooms really clearly, like the smell of those dry-erase markers and this one teacher who had a huge collection of turtle figurines, which… not really what you asked.\n\nHomework, I mean, I probably procrastinated like a lot of kids did, but I don’t know if it was more than normal. So no, I haven’t really noticed trouble staying still like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your difficulty focusing or getting things done is often accompanied by feeling down, losing interest in things you usually enjoy, or feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s tied to that, but maybe more like I get overwhelmed first, and then I end up feeling really low after. I came in because ever since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business, I keep getting so wound up over things like invoices or client messages.\n\nIt’s stupid little stuff, or it feels like it should be little, like replying to someone or figuring out what to charge, and then I avoid it until I’m sitting at my kitchen table crying. And then I’ll forget normal things too, like paying a bill, because my brain is just stuck on this one client email. So, yes, the focus and getting things done part is happening with feeling down, but it feels like it starts from the stress of all of that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, like making art or other hobbies, especially since these difficulties started?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 15\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The question is specifically asking about loss of interest or pleasure in usual activities, which is not covered by the provided symptom profile manifestations."}

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure. I mean, art still matters to me, if that makes sense. It’s not like I look at my supplies and feel nothing.

But sometimes when it turns into the business side of it, it kind of… drains the fun out of the room. Like I’ll sit down thinking I’m going to work on something, and then I notice the pile of receipts or remember some email, and suddenly I’m rearranging brushes for twenty minutes. I have this one jar of old brushes I keep meaning to clean out—my friend says it looks “artistically tragic,” which made me laugh way too hard.

So… I don’t know if I’ve lost interest exactly. I think I still enjoy it when I can actually get into it. It’s just harder to get there lately.

Like, the art part is fine, mostly, but then there are invoices and client messages and all these little business things, and I just get so wound up about them.\n\nAnd then I avoid them, which makes it worse. I’ll tell myself I’m going to answer one email after I make coffee, and then somehow I’m reorganizing my brushes or looking up packaging ideas, and then it’s hours later. I’ve ended up crying at my kitchen table over it, which feels ridiculous. And I’ve been forgetting normal things too, like paying a bill, because I’m so tangled up in worrying about the client stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or avoidance is mainly around interacting with clients or presenting yourself professionally, or does it also happen with other types of social interactions?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the client and business side, I think. Like, if I’m just talking to a friend or chatting with someone at the grocery store, that’s not really the same. But when it’s a client message or an invoice, or I have to sound professional, I get so wound up and then I put it off.\n\nAnd then I’ll do this whole thing where I tell myself I’m being productive because I’m, I don’t know, cleaning my art desk or looking at envelope sizes online, but really I’m avoiding the email. I’ve even forgotten normal stuff, like paying a bill, because I’m so caught up in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a sense of inner restlessness or tension when you're faced with these business tasks, like you can't sit still or focus until you get started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… it’s like I get wound up before I even open the message. That’s kind of why I came in—since trying to make my art into a real freelance thing, these tasks keep turning into this whole spiral where I avoid them until I’m crying at the kitchen table or I forget normal stuff, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to focus or getting easily distracted by other things, even when you’re trying to do something that isn’t related to your freelance work, like chores or reading a book?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I mean, everybody gets distracted doing chores, right? Like I’ll start folding laundry and then notice the plant needs water, and then I’m looking for the little watering can I bought at this thrift store—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it being a big problem when it’s just normal stuff like reading or chores. It feels more obvious when it’s the freelance business things. But I don’t know, maybe I’m not paying close enough attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your freelance work, do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like following through with detailed instructions or staying organized with everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really tracked it that way. Like, with everyday things, I can usually muddle through, I think? If there are a lot of steps I might get annoyed or wander off for a minute, but that feels kind of normal.\n\nI did recently try to organize this drawer with all my receipts and random batteries and, somehow, old birthday candles—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess outside the freelance stuff, it doesn’t jump out to me as a major problem. It’s more that I notice it when there’s some business-y task hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, even before you started your freelance business, did you often struggle with staying organized, starting tasks, or avoiding things that needed to be done?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like this. I mean, I’ve always had little messy piles and I’m not the most organized person—my desk has, like, three different mugs on it right now, which is embarrassing—but it wasn’t this whole overwhelmed, avoidant thing.\n\nIt really started maybe about a month after I took on that community arts contract and was trying to make the business more… official, I guess. Like once it felt formal and there were all these business tasks attached, that’s when I started freezing up and putting things off.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, even before starting your freelance business, did you ever notice that you would often lose things, forget daily tasks, or struggle to follow through on things you started?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in a way that stands out before all this. I’d forget little things sometimes, like anyone, and I’ve definitely misplaced my keys in weird places—once they were in a grocery bag with onions, don’t ask—but it didn’t feel like a pattern.\n\nThe overwhelmed, avoidant thing really started about a month after I took on that community arts contract and tried to make the business more formal. That’s when I started feeling like I’d freeze up around those tasks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty starting or finishing tasks that aren't related to your freelance work—like personal projects, hobbies, or even simple routines like getting ready in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little… I mean, the main thing is still the freelance stuff, but it sort of spills over. Like I put off replying to a client email for way too long, and then I felt awful about it, and at home I missed a utility payment, which is not really like me.\n\nI was trying to keep up with the art display at the same time, and everything just felt tangled together—like the email, the bill, the display labels, all of it. Personal routines aren’t impossible, exactly, but the responsibilities around home and work are getting harder to start or finish.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems managing daily responsibilities like personal hygiene, meals, or household chores because of how overwhelmed or distracted you feel?\n\nPatient: Um… hygiene and meals, not so much, I don’t think. Like, I’m still showering and eating, though sometimes dinner becomes crackers and cheese because I get distracted by one more thing—actually I bought this fancy mustard at the farmers market and now everything is “dinner” if it has mustard on it, which is probably not the point.\n\nBut the household and responsibility stuff, yes. It’s started affecting both the freelance work and home stuff. I put off replying to a client email way too long, and then I missed a utility payment while I was also trying to keep up with the art display. It’s like all the tasks pile up and I just… stall.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your freelance work, do you find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require paying close attention, like following detailed directions or keeping track of multiple steps in a plan?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it’s kind of wrapped up in everything else. Like, outside the actual freelance work, home responsibilities have started slipping too. I missed a utility payment, which made me feel really stupid because it was just sitting there, and I kept meaning to deal with it.\n\nAnd then with the art display, there were all these little pieces to keep track of, and at the same time I was avoiding replying to a client email. It’s like if there are multiple steps, my brain starts treating it like a junk drawer—actually I have one of those with batteries and old museum wristbands in it—and I just don’t know what to grab first. So yeah, it’s affecting both work and home stuff now.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, were you often distractible or had trouble staying focused on tasks or activities when you were a child, like in school or while doing homework?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember it being a big thing when I was a kid, but honestly my memory of school is kind of patchy in that way. I remember certain classrooms really clearly, like the smell of those dry-erase markers and this one teacher who had a huge collection of turtle figurines, which… not really what you asked.\n\nHomework, I mean, I probably procrastinated like a lot of kids did, but I don’t know if it was more than normal. Nothing really stands out to me as, “Oh, I was always distracted back then.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks or feeling stuck even when there’s no pressure from others or professional consequences, like personal errands or simple decisions such as what to wear or what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s showing up more when there are responsibilities stacked together, not just if someone is standing over me. And I’ve been forgetting normal things too, like paying a bill, because I’m so tangled up in worrying about the client stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or avoidance is mainly around interacting with clients or presenting yourself professionally, or does it also happen with other types of social interactions?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the client and business side, I think. Like, if I’m just talking to a friend or chatting with someone at the grocery store, that’s not really the same. But when it’s a client message or an invoice, or I have to sound professional, I get so wound up and then I put it off.\n\nAnd then I’ll do this whole thing where I tell myself I’m being productive because I’m, I don’t know, cleaning my art desk or looking at envelope sizes online, but really I’m avoiding the email. Then suddenly it’s late and I’m crying at the kitchen table because it feels like such a stupid thing to be stuck on. I’ve even forgotten normal stuff, like paying a bill, because I’m so caught up in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a sense of inner restlessness or tension when you're faced with these business tasks, like you can't sit still or focus until you get started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… it’s like I get wound up before I even open the message. I’ll know I need to do an invoice or reply to a client, and instead of just doing it I sort of buzz around doing other things, like rearranging supplies or checking some totally random business thing that doesn’t matter right then.\n\nAnd then it builds up, because I haven’t actually done the thing. That’s kind of why I came in—since trying to make my art into a real freelance thing, these tasks keep turning into this whole spiral where I avoid them until I’m crying at the kitchen table or I forget normal stuff, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to focus or getting easily distracted by other things, even when you’re trying to do something that isn’t related to your freelance work, like chores or reading a book?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I mean, everybody gets distracted doing chores, right? Like I’ll start folding laundry and then notice the plant needs water, and then I’m looking for the little watering can I bought at this thrift store—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it being a big problem when it’s just normal stuff like reading or chores. It feels more obvious when it’s the freelance business things. But I don’t know, maybe I’m not paying close enough attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your freelance work, do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like following through with detailed instructions or staying organized with everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really tracked it that way. Like, with everyday things, I can usually muddle through, I think? If there are a lot of steps I might get annoyed or wander off for a minute, but that feels kind of normal.\n\nI did recently try to organize this drawer with all my receipts and random batteries and, somehow, old birthday candles—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess outside the freelance stuff, it doesn’t jump out to me as a major problem. It’s more that I notice it when there’s some business-y task hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, even before you started your freelance business, did you often struggle with staying organized, starting tasks, or avoiding things that needed to be done?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like this. I mean, I’ve always had little messy piles and I’m not the most organized person—my desk has, like, three different mugs on it right now, which is embarrassing—but it wasn’t this whole overwhelmed, avoidant thing.\n\nIt really started maybe about a month after I took on that community arts contract and was trying to make the business more… official, I guess. Like once it felt formal and there were all these business tasks attached, that’s when I started freezing up and putting things off.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, even before starting your freelance business, did you ever notice that you would often lose things, forget daily tasks, or struggle to follow through on things you started?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in a way that stands out before all this. I’d forget little things sometimes, like anyone, and I’ve definitely misplaced my keys in weird places—once they were in a grocery bag with onions, don’t ask—but it didn’t feel like a pattern.\n\nThe overwhelmed, avoidant thing really started about a month after I took on that community arts contract and tried to make the business more formal. That’s when I started feeling like I’d freeze up around those tasks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty starting or finishing tasks that aren't related to your freelance work—like personal projects, hobbies, or even simple routines like getting ready in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little… I mean, the main thing is still the freelance stuff, but it sort of spills over. Like I put off replying to a client email for way too long, and then I felt awful about it, and at home I missed a utility payment, which is not really like me.\n\nI was trying to keep up with the art display at the same time, and everything just felt tangled together—like the email, the bill, the display labels, all of it. Personal routines aren’t impossible, exactly, but the responsibilities around home and work are getting harder to start or finish.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems managing daily responsibilities like personal hygiene, meals, or household chores because of how overwhelmed or distracted you feel?\n\nPatient: Um… hygiene and meals, not so much, I don’t think. Like, I’m still showering and eating, though sometimes dinner becomes crackers and cheese because I get distracted by one more thing—actually I bought this fancy mustard at the farmers market and now everything is “dinner” if it has mustard on it, which is probably not the point.\n\nBut the household and responsibility stuff, yes. It’s started affecting both the freelance work and home stuff. I put off replying to a client email way too long, and then I missed a utility payment while I was also trying to keep up with the art display. It’s like all the tasks pile up and I just… stall.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your freelance work, do you find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require paying close attention, like following detailed directions or keeping track of multiple steps in a plan?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it’s kind of wrapped up in everything else. Like, outside the actual freelance work, home responsibilities have started slipping too. I missed a utility payment, which made me feel really stupid because it was just sitting there, and I kept meaning to deal with it.\n\nAnd then with the art display, there were all these little pieces to keep track of, and at the same time I was avoiding replying to a client email. It’s like if there are multiple steps, my brain starts treating it like a junk drawer—actually I have one of those with batteries and old museum wristbands in it—and I just don’t know what to grab first. So yeah, it’s affecting both work and home stuff now.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, were you often distractible or had trouble staying focused on tasks or activities when you were a child, like in school or while doing homework?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember it being a big thing when I was a kid, but honestly my memory of school is kind of patchy in that way. I remember certain classrooms really clearly, like the smell of those dry-erase markers and this one teacher who had a huge collection of turtle figurines, which… not really what you asked.\n\nHomework, I mean, I probably procrastinated like a lot of kids did, but I don’t know if it was more than normal. Nothing really stands out to me as, “Oh, I was always distracted back then.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks or feeling stuck even when there’s no pressure from others or professional consequences, like personal errands or simple decisions such as what to wear or what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s showing up more when there are responsibilities stacked together, not just if someone is standing over me. Like I put off replying to a client way longer than I should have, and then I missed a utility payment, which was just sitting there needing to be done.\n\nAnd all of that was while I was trying to keep up with the art display stuff, which has all these tiny moving pieces—labels, timing, where things go, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I need better tape or if the frames are crooked. So I don’t know if it’s “simple decisions” exactly, but I do get stuck, and then things at home and with work start slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or have trouble staying still, even when you’re not facing any specific task or responsibility?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a big way. I mean, I shift around sometimes, like if a chair is uncomfortable or I’m waiting somewhere and there’s a weird clock ticking—there was one at my aunt’s house that was so loud it felt like it was arguing with the room—but I wouldn’t say I’m usually physically restless for no reason.\n\nI’m more… I don’t know, I can sit. Sometimes probably too much. So no, I haven’t really noticed trouble staying still like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your difficulty focusing or getting things done is often accompanied by feeling down, losing interest in things you usually enjoy, or feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s tied to that, but maybe more like I get overwhelmed first, and then I end up feeling really low after. I came in because ever since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business, I keep getting so wound up over things like invoices or client messages.\n\nIt’s stupid little stuff, or it feels like it should be little, like replying to someone or figuring out what to charge, and then I avoid it until I’m sitting at my kitchen table crying. And then I’ll forget normal things too, like paying a bill, because my brain is just stuck on this one client email. So, yes, the focus and getting things done part is happening with feeling down, but it feels like it starts from the stress of all of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, like making art or other hobbies, especially since these difficulties started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, art still matters to me, if that makes sense. It’s not like I look at my supplies and feel nothing.\n\nBut sometimes when it turns into the business side of it, it kind of… drains the fun out of the room. Like I’ll sit down thinking I’m going to work on something, and then I notice the pile of receipts or remember some email, and suddenly I’m rearranging brushes for twenty minutes. I have this one jar of old brushes I keep meaning to clean out—my friend says it looks “artistically tragic,” which made me laugh way too hard.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve lost interest exactly. I think I still enjoy it when I can actually get into it. It’s just harder to get there lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, especially since these difficulties with your freelance work started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day, nearly every day. It’s more like… it hits around the freelance stuff. Like ever since I tried to make my art into an actual business, I get so wound up over invoices and client messages, and then I avoid them.\n\nAnd then suddenly it’s me at the kitchen table crying because I still haven’t answered one email. The table has this little wobble too, which makes everything feel more annoying than it should—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut yeah, I do feel really down in those moments. Hopeless maybe, like I can’t handle normal adult things. And then I’ll forget something regular, like paying a bill, because I’m stuck thinking about that one client message. So it’s not constant all day, but it’s been happening around this whole freelance thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like once it felt formal and there were all these business tasks attached, that’s when I started freezing up and putting things off.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, even before starting your freelance business, did you ever notice that you would often lose things, forget daily tasks, or struggle to follow through on things you started?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in a way that stands out before all this. That’s when I started feeling like I’d freeze up around those tasks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty starting or finishing tasks that aren't related to your freelance work—like personal projects, hobbies, or even simple routines like getting ready in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little… I mean, the main thing is still the freelance stuff, but it sort of spills over. Like I put off replying to a client email for way too long, and then I felt awful about it, and at home I missed a utility payment, which is not really like me.\n\nI was trying to keep up with the art display at the same time, and everything just felt tangled together—like the email, the bill, the display labels, all of it. Personal routines aren’t impossible, exactly, but the responsibilities around home and work are getting harder to start or finish.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems managing daily responsibilities like personal hygiene, meals, or household chores because of how overwhelmed or distracted you feel?\n\nPatient: Um… hygiene and meals, not so much, I don’t think. Like, I’m still showering and eating, though sometimes dinner becomes crackers and cheese because I get distracted by one more thing—actually I bought this fancy mustard at the farmers market and now everything is “dinner” if it has mustard on it, which is probably not the point.\n\nBut the household and responsibility stuff, yes. It’s started affecting both the freelance work and home stuff. I put off replying to a client email way too long, and then I missed a utility payment while I was also trying to keep up with the art display. It’s like all the tasks pile up and I just… stall.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your freelance work, do you find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require paying close attention, like following detailed directions or keeping track of multiple steps in a plan?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it’s kind of wrapped up in everything else. Like, outside the actual freelance work, home responsibilities have started slipping too. I missed a utility payment, which made me feel really stupid because it was just sitting there, and I kept meaning to deal with it.\n\nAnd then with the art display, there were all these little pieces to keep track of, and at the same time I was avoiding replying to a client email. It’s like if there are multiple steps, my brain starts treating it like a junk drawer—actually I have one of those with batteries and old museum wristbands in it—and I just don’t know what to grab first. So yeah, it’s affecting both work and home stuff now.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, were you often distractible or had trouble staying focused on tasks or activities when you were a child, like in school or while doing homework?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember it being a big thing when I was a kid, but honestly my memory of school is kind of patchy in that way. I remember certain classrooms really clearly, like the smell of those dry-erase markers and this one teacher who had a huge collection of turtle figurines, which… not really what you asked.\n\nHomework, I mean, I probably procrastinated like a lot of kids did, but I don’t know if it was more than normal. Nothing really stands out to me as, “Oh, I was always distracted back then.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks or feeling stuck even when there’s no pressure from others or professional consequences, like personal errands or simple decisions such as what to wear or what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s showing up more when there are responsibilities stacked together, not just if someone is standing over me. Like I put off replying to a client way longer than I should have, and then I missed a utility payment, which was just sitting there needing to be done.\n\nAnd all of that was while I was trying to keep up with the art display stuff, which has all these tiny moving pieces—labels, timing, where things go, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I need better tape or if the frames are crooked. So I don’t know if it’s “simple decisions” exactly, but I do get stuck, and then things at home and with work start slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or have trouble staying still, even when you’re not facing any specific task or responsibility?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a big way. I mean, I shift around sometimes, like if a chair is uncomfortable or I’m waiting somewhere and there’s a weird clock ticking—there was one at my aunt’s house that was so loud it felt like it was arguing with the room—but I wouldn’t say I’m usually physically restless for no reason.\n\nI’m more… I don’t know, I can sit. Sometimes probably too much. So no, I haven’t really noticed trouble staying still like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your difficulty focusing or getting things done is often accompanied by feeling down, losing interest in things you usually enjoy, or feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s tied to that, but maybe more like I get overwhelmed first, and then I end up feeling really low after. I came in because ever since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business, I keep getting so wound up over things like invoices or client messages.\n\nIt’s stupid little stuff, or it feels like it should be little, like replying to someone or figuring out what to charge, and then I avoid it until I’m sitting at my kitchen table crying. And then I’ll forget normal things too, like paying a bill, because my brain is just stuck on this one client email. So, yes, the focus and getting things done part is happening with feeling down, but it feels like it starts from the stress of all of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, like making art or other hobbies, especially since these difficulties started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, art still matters to me, if that makes sense. It’s not like I look at my supplies and feel nothing.\n\nBut sometimes when it turns into the business side of it, it kind of… drains the fun out of the room. Like I’ll sit down thinking I’m going to work on something, and then I notice the pile of receipts or remember some email, and suddenly I’m rearranging brushes for twenty minutes. I have this one jar of old brushes I keep meaning to clean out—my friend says it looks “artistically tragic,” which made me laugh way too hard.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve lost interest exactly. I think I still enjoy it when I can actually get into it. It’s just harder to get there lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, especially since these difficulties with your freelance work started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day, nearly every day. It’s more like… it hits around the freelance stuff. Like ever since I tried to make my art into an actual business, I get so wound up over invoices and client messages, and then I avoid them.\n\nAnd then suddenly it’s me at the kitchen table crying because I still haven’t answered one email. The table has this little wobble too, which makes everything feel more annoying than it should—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut yeah, I do feel really down in those moments. Hopeless maybe, like I can’t handle normal adult things. And then I’ll forget something regular, like paying a bill, because I’m stuck thinking about that one client message. So it’s not constant all day, but it’s been happening around this whole freelance thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t take as much pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, even outside of the stress around your freelance work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed it outside of the freelance stress, honestly. Like… if I’m away from that whole mess, I can still enjoy things, I think.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll go for coffee with a friend or watch some ridiculous home renovation show and feel fine. Although those shows always make me think I could redo my bathroom, which is a terrible idea because I once painted one wall and somehow got paint on the ceiling fan—don’t ask.\n\nBut yeah, outside of the work stuff, I don’t think I’ve totally lost interest in things. It’s more that the stress sort of follows me around sometimes, so it’s hard to tell.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s not like I look at my supplies and feel nothing.\n\nBut sometimes when it turns into the business side of it, it kind of… drains the fun out of the room. Like I’ll sit down thinking I’m going to work on something, and then I notice the pile of receipts or remember some email, and suddenly I’m rearranging brushes for twenty minutes. I have this one jar of old brushes I keep meaning to clean out—my friend says it looks “artistically tragic,” which made me laugh way too hard.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve lost interest exactly. I think I still enjoy it when I can actually get into it. It’s just harder to get there lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, especially since these difficulties with your freelance work started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day, nearly every day. It’s more like… it hits around the freelance stuff. Like ever since I tried to make my art into an actual business, I get so wound up over invoices and client messages, and then I avoid them.\n\nAnd then suddenly it’s me at the kitchen table crying because I still haven’t answered one email. The table has this little wobble too, which makes everything feel more annoying than it should—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut yeah, I do feel really down in those moments. Hopeless maybe, like I can’t handle normal adult things. And then I’ll forget something regular, like paying a bill, because I’m stuck thinking about that one client message. So it’s not constant all day, but it’s been happening around this whole freelance thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t take as much pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, even outside of the stress around your freelance work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed it outside of the freelance stress, honestly. Like… if I’m away from that whole mess, I can still enjoy things, I think.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll go for coffee with a friend or watch some ridiculous home renovation show and feel fine. Although those shows always make me think I could redo my bathroom, which is a terrible idea because I once painted one wall and somehow got paint on the ceiling fan—don’t ask.\n\nBut yeah, outside of the work stuff, I don’t think I’ve totally lost interest in things. It’s more that the stress sort of follows me around sometimes, so it’s hard to tell.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about your freelance work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think… I don’t know that I can separate it that cleanly. Like, when I’m not actively thinking about freelance stuff, I can still enjoy things. I’ll get into a book or laugh at something dumb with a friend.

But then sometimes my brain sort of loops back to it anyway, like I’ll be watching a movie and suddenly remember I never bought printer paper, which is ridiculous because I don’t even print that much anymore. I used to have this ancient printer that sounded like it was grinding coffee beans, and honestly I kind of miss it.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of pleasure when the work stress isn’t in the background. It’s more that the background is loud sometimes.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve been getting really overwhelmed since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business. Like, the art part is fine, mostly, but then there are invoices and client messages and all these little business things, and I just get so wound up about them.\n\nAnd then I avoid them, which makes it worse. I’ll tell myself I’m going to answer one email after I make coffee, and then somehow I’m reorganizing my brushes or looking up packaging ideas, and then it’s hours later. I’ve ended up crying at my kitchen table over it, which feels ridiculous. And I’ve been forgetting normal things too, like paying a bill, because I’m so tangled up in worrying about the client stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or avoidance is mainly around interacting with clients or presenting yourself professionally, or does it also happen with other types of social interactions?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the client and business side, I think. Like, if I’m just talking to a friend or chatting with someone at the grocery store, that’s not really the same. But when it’s a client message or an invoice, or I have to sound professional, I get so wound up and then I put it off.\n\nAnd then I’ll do this whole thing where I tell myself I’m being productive because I’m, I don’t know, cleaning my art desk or looking at envelope sizes online, but really I’m avoiding the email. Then suddenly it’s late and I’m crying at the kitchen table because it feels like such a stupid thing to be stuck on. I’ve even forgotten normal stuff, like paying a bill, because I’m so caught up in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a sense of inner restlessness or tension when you're faced with these business tasks, like you can't sit still or focus until you get started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… it’s like I get wound up before I even open the message. I’ll know I need to do an invoice or reply to a client, and instead of just doing it I sort of buzz around doing other things, like rearranging supplies or checking some totally random business thing that doesn’t matter right then.\n\nAnd then it builds up, because I haven’t actually done the thing. That’s kind of why I came in—since trying to make my art into a real freelance thing, these tasks keep turning into this whole spiral where I avoid them until I’m crying at the kitchen table or I forget normal stuff, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to focus or getting easily distracted by other things, even when you’re trying to do something that isn’t related to your freelance work, like chores or reading a book?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I mean, everybody gets distracted doing chores, right? Like I’ll start folding laundry and then notice the plant needs water, and then I’m looking for the little watering can I bought at this thrift store—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it being a big problem when it’s just normal stuff like reading or chores. It feels more obvious when it’s the freelance business things. But I don’t know, maybe I’m not paying close enough attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your freelance work, do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like following through with detailed instructions or staying organized with everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really tracked it that way. Like, with everyday things, I can usually muddle through, I think? If there are a lot of steps I might get annoyed or wander off for a minute, but that feels kind of normal.\n\nI did recently try to organize this drawer with all my receipts and random batteries and, somehow, old birthday candles—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess outside the freelance stuff, it doesn’t jump out to me as a major problem. It’s more that I notice it when there’s some business-y task hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, even before you started your freelance business, did you often struggle with staying organized, starting tasks, or avoiding things that needed to be done?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like this. I mean, I’ve always had little messy piles and I’m not the most organized person—my desk has, like, three different mugs on it right now, which is embarrassing—but it wasn’t this whole overwhelmed, avoidant thing.\n\nIt really started maybe about a month after I took on that community arts contract and was trying to make the business more… official, I guess. Like once it felt formal and there were all these business tasks attached, that’s when I started freezing up and putting things off.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, even before starting your freelance business, did you ever notice that you would often lose things, forget daily tasks, or struggle to follow through on things you started?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in a way that stands out before all this. I’d forget little things sometimes, like anyone, and I’ve definitely misplaced my keys in weird places—once they were in a grocery bag with onions, don’t ask—but it didn’t feel like a pattern.\n\nThe overwhelmed, avoidant thing really started about a month after I took on that community arts contract and tried to make the business more formal. That’s when I started feeling like I’d freeze up around those tasks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty starting or finishing tasks that aren't related to your freelance work—like personal projects, hobbies, or even simple routines like getting ready in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little… I mean, the main thing is still the freelance stuff, but it sort of spills over. Like I put off replying to a client email for way too long, and then I felt awful about it, and at home I missed a utility payment, which is not really like me.\n\nI was trying to keep up with the art display at the same time, and everything just felt tangled together—like the email, the bill, the display labels, all of it. Personal routines aren’t impossible, exactly, but the responsibilities around home and work are getting harder to start or finish.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems managing daily responsibilities like personal hygiene, meals, or household chores because of how overwhelmed or distracted you feel?\n\nPatient: Um… hygiene and meals, not so much, I don’t think. Like, I’m still showering and eating, though sometimes dinner becomes crackers and cheese because I get distracted by one more thing—actually I bought this fancy mustard at the farmers market and now everything is “dinner” if it has mustard on it, which is probably not the point.\n\nBut the household and responsibility stuff, yes. It’s started affecting both the freelance work and home stuff. I put off replying to a client email way too long, and then I missed a utility payment while I was also trying to keep up with the art display. It’s like all the tasks pile up and I just… stall.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your freelance work, do you find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require paying close attention, like following detailed directions or keeping track of multiple steps in a plan?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it’s kind of wrapped up in everything else. Like, outside the actual freelance work, home responsibilities have started slipping too. I missed a utility payment, which made me feel really stupid because it was just sitting there, and I kept meaning to deal with it.\n\nAnd then with the art display, there were all these little pieces to keep track of, and at the same time I was avoiding replying to a client email. It’s like if there are multiple steps, my brain starts treating it like a junk drawer—actually I have one of those with batteries and old museum wristbands in it—and I just don’t know what to grab first. So yeah, it’s affecting both work and home stuff now.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, were you often distractible or had trouble staying focused on tasks or activities when you were a child, like in school or while doing homework?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember it being a big thing when I was a kid, but honestly my memory of school is kind of patchy in that way. I remember certain classrooms really clearly, like the smell of those dry-erase markers and this one teacher who had a huge collection of turtle figurines, which… not really what you asked.\n\nHomework, I mean, I probably procrastinated like a lot of kids did, but I don’t know if it was more than normal. Nothing really stands out to me as, “Oh, I was always distracted back then.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks or feeling stuck even when there’s no pressure from others or professional consequences, like personal errands or simple decisions such as what to wear or what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s showing up more when there are responsibilities stacked together, not just if someone is standing over me. Like I put off replying to a client way longer than I should have, and then I missed a utility payment, which was just sitting there needing to be done.\n\nAnd all of that was while I was trying to keep up with the art display stuff, which has all these tiny moving pieces—labels, timing, where things go, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I need better tape or if the frames are crooked. So I don’t know if it’s “simple decisions” exactly, but I do get stuck, and then things at home and with work start slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or have trouble staying still, even when you’re not facing any specific task or responsibility?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a big way. I mean, I shift around sometimes, like if a chair is uncomfortable or I’m waiting somewhere and there’s a weird clock ticking—there was one at my aunt’s house that was so loud it felt like it was arguing with the room—but I wouldn’t say I’m usually physically restless for no reason.\n\nI’m more… I don’t know, I can sit. Sometimes probably too much. So no, I haven’t really noticed trouble staying still like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your difficulty focusing or getting things done is often accompanied by feeling down, losing interest in things you usually enjoy, or feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s tied to that, but maybe more like I get overwhelmed first, and then I end up feeling really low after. I came in because ever since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business, I keep getting so wound up over things like invoices or client messages.\n\nIt’s stupid little stuff, or it feels like it should be little, like replying to someone or figuring out what to charge, and then I avoid it until I’m sitting at my kitchen table crying. And then I’ll forget normal things too, like paying a bill, because my brain is just stuck on this one client email. So, yes, the focus and getting things done part is happening with feeling down, but it feels like it starts from the stress of all of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, like making art or other hobbies, especially since these difficulties started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, art still matters to me, if that makes sense. It’s not like I look at my supplies and feel nothing.\n\nBut sometimes when it turns into the business side of it, it kind of… drains the fun out of the room. Like I’ll sit down thinking I’m going to work on something, and then I notice the pile of receipts or remember some email, and suddenly I’m rearranging brushes for twenty minutes. I have this one jar of old brushes I keep meaning to clean out—my friend says it looks “artistically tragic,” which made me laugh way too hard.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve lost interest exactly. I think I still enjoy it when I can actually get into it. It’s just harder to get there lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, especially since these difficulties with your freelance work started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day, nearly every day.
